App. 108 


neutralize the economic incentive to violate environmen- 
tal regulations.” Id. at 264; see also Smithfield Foods, __ 
F.3d at __, 1999 WL 713847, at *12; Pub. Interest Research 
Group of N.J., 913 F.2d at 80. “[T]he precise economic 
benefit a polluter has gained by violating [the Act] may 
be difficult to prove, so ‘[rjeasonable approximations of 
economic benefit will suffice.’ ” Smithfield Foods, 1999 WL 
713847, at *12 (quoting and altering S. Rep. No. 50, 99th 
Cong., 1st Sess. 25 (1985)); see also Sierra Club, Lone Star 
Chapter, 73 F.3d at 576; Pub. Interest Research Group of N.]., 
913 F.2d at 80. The EPA suggests that “Tsakopoulos bene- 
fitted economically in several ways as a result of proceed- 
ing with deep ripping without obtaining a permit.” EPA’s 
Trial Brief at 32. The EPA argues he avoided the cost of 
pursing authorization to deep rip the jurisdictional fea- 
tures and mitigation costs that would have been required 
by the government for him to receive a permit. Id. at 33. 
Second, he saved interest costs that would have accrued 
had he gone through with the permitting process and, 
resultantly, not been able to sell parcels 6, 8, 9, and 10 as 
quickly as he did. Third, he received profit from those 
sales sooner than he would have if a permit application 
had been properly submitted and processed. Id. The 
EPA’s economics expert testified that the total economic 
benefit, obtained by adding these (and certain other) 
elements together, equals $1,039,628.!7 Also, the EPA 


17 According to the EPA's expert, from avoiding the cost of 
mitigation, Tsakopoulos benefitted by holding this money 
himself for an economic benefit of $416,687. From early sales, he 
benefitted $601,359; and from failing to properly flag 
jurisdictional wetlands on parcel 10 prior to deep ripping, he 
saved $21,582. 


App. 109 


points to the tremendous profit that Tsakopoulos has 
earned as a result of his conversion activities on Borden 
Ranch. Tsakopoulos purchased parcels 6, 8, 9, and 10 for 
$605,323 and has since sold this land for $3,123,914. How- 
ever, he incurred approximately $673,000 in land prepara- 
tion, selling, interest, and other costs in the interim.'® 


Tsakopoulos argues that he has not benefitted eco- 
nomically at all from the violations. The properties at 
issue were converted for use as vineyards and then sold 
at a price per gross acre, rather than per farmable acre, so 
that there was no economic incentive to fill the wetlands, 
and no benefit from doing so. Tsakopoulos’s Trial Brief at 
54. Tsakopoulos also points out that he did expend some 
additional time and money in attempting to comply with 
the Clean Water Act. Id. 


The acreage involved in these violations constitutes a 
minuscule fraction of the total land converted to vine- 
yards in parcels 6, 8, 9, and 10. Therefore, deeming his 
profits from the sale of these parcels to be the economic 
benefit derived solely from his violations would not 
reflect the extent of his economic gain. But Tsakopoulos 
did save money by selling the relevant parcels earlier 
than he would have realized had he taken the time to 
comply with the Act. It is clear that he moved with 


18 Also, according to the EPA’s expert, Tsakopoulos has 
sold half of the land on Borden Ranch for approximately $17 
million, and the remaining portion is worth approximately $7.7 
million. Thus, Tsakopoulos stands to make a gross profit of 
roughly $16.4 million from the Borden Ranch deal, without 
taking into account conversion, interest, and other costs. 


App. 110 


celerity in his deep ripping activities because of the pros- 
pect of making an enormous profit, the immediate avail- 
ability of this profit, and the ability to avoid expense and 
delay he perceived attended to action that would ensure 
compliance with the Clean Water Act. Thus he risked 
damaging rare federal wetlands because of his motivation 
to reap economic gain. This factor favors a substantial 
penalty, but the extent of the economic benefit is uncer- 
tain. Where the precise economic benefit is difficult to 
determine, “reasonable approximations of economic ben- 
efit will suffice.” Pub. Interest Research Group of N.J., 913 
F.2d at 80. 


C. 
Any History of Such Violations 


The parties dispute what should be considered when 
evaluating the history Tsakopoulos has had with the Act 
and what weight should be given to that history. 
Tsakopoulos’s past alleged violations of the Act include 
those committed prior to 1993 at sites other than Borden 
Ranch, those committed on the Castlehill parcel in the fall 
of 1993, those committed on the Prudential parcel in the 
fall of 1994, and others committed on the Sacramento 
County side of Borden Ranch in 1995. Tsakopoulos points 
out that the AOC entered into on May 3, 1996, fully 
considered these violations and reached a settlement that 
sufficiently penalized Tsakopoulos even though he was 
not required to admit wrongdoing. Tsakopoulos indicates 
that the AOC fully required him to make amends for 
what occurred on the Sacramento County side of Borden 


App. 111 


Ranch, and reflects a penalty equal to what the govern- 
ment was likely to have obtained had the matter been 
fully prosecuted instead of settled. When the EPA consid- 
ered an appropriate penalty and mitigation in the AOC, 
and extracted from Tsakopoulos 1,368 acres of land for 
preserves and nearly $45,000, the EPA must have consid- 
ered and intended to resolve the Sacramento County and 
all prior violations of the Clean Water Act (and violations 
of the Endangered Species Act) that Tsakopoulos had 
committed. While his history is problematic, the federal 
government dealt with it and appears to have fully con- 
sidered it in the AOC. The EPA disagrees, contending 
that since Tsakopoulos committed further violations of 
the Act he “vitiated” the AOC by breaching the promise 
he made in the AOC that he would not engage in further 
violations. See Exh. 66 at 11. Therefore, the EPA argues 
that the parties’ settlement reflected in the AOC should 
be disregarded and the Court should begin anew when 
considering a penalty for Tsakopoulos’s current viola- 
tions in light of similar violations he allegedly committed 
in the past. 


In determining the history of all of the violations, the 
Court is required to consider the duration and nature of 
them all, “including whether the violations are perpetual 
or sporadic.” United States v. Smithfield Foods, 972 F. Supp. 
338, 349 (E.D.Va.1997) (citing cases), rev'd in part on other 
grounds, ___ F.3d __, 1999 WL 713847 (4th Cir. Sept. 14, 
1999). A review of the AOC, in light of the trial testimony 
concerning Tsakopoulos’s history, reflects Tsakopoulos 
had problems complying with the requirements of the 
Clean Water Act, but leads to the conclusion that the 
penalty under consideration now should supplement 


App. 112 


rather than supplant the governmental enforcement 
action reflected in the AOC. The AOC represents a final 
agency enforcement action that evinces the government 
utilized considerable expertise as it diligently concluded 
an administrative agreement with Tsakopoulos which fos- 
ters the goals of the Act. No reason has been presented 
justifying undoing the agreement reached in the AOC. 
However, Tsakopoulos’s violation of the AOC itself by 
reneging on his assurance to comply with the Act is of 
historical significance and weighs in favor of a more 
severe penalty. Although all of Tsakopoulos’s past history 
is considered, it is not evaluated in the light desired by 


the EPA. 


d. 


Any Good-Faith Efforts to Comply 
with Applicable Requirements 


The next factor concerns whether Tsakopoulos made 
good faith efforts to comply with the Clean Water Act. 
This factor generally focuses on whether the violator took 
any actions to decrease the number of violations and 
whether he made an effort to mitigate the impact of his 
violations. Smithfield Foods, 972 F. Supp. at 350 (citing 


cases). 


The EPA contends that Tsakopoulos undertook vir- 
tually no efforts to comply with the Clean Water Act and 
regulations promulgated thereunder, disregarded the 
government’s warnings and “chose to go forward in San 
Joaquin County without a permit and in a manner that 
essentially assured that the rippers would impact juris- 
dictional waters.” EPA’s Trial Brief at 34, 35. The evidence 


App. 113 


indicates that Tsakopoulos’s agents utilized the deep rip- 
ping equipment almost 24 hours per day with little or no 
supervision at night, and in a manner which seemed 
impervious to the substantial risk of ripping and deposi- 
ting fill into federal wetlands. These facts strongly indi- 
cate a lack of good faith. 


Tsakopoulos points out that he told the government 
about his plans for Borden Ranch as soon as he realized 
he would purchase the property and there was evidence 
that he made some efforts to mark jurisdictional features. 
However, the EPA correctly asserts that Tsakopoulos 
repeatedly failed to adhere to the regulatory scheme and 
occasionally acted as though he did not recognize the 
authority of the Corps and the EPA in his dealings with 
them. And although Tsakopoulos quibbled with the 
Corps’s enforcement authority, he knew that the Corps 
had clearly directed him not to deep-rip federal wetlands. 
However, the evidence does reveal that Tsakopoulos 
apparently intended to avoid the necessity of obtaining a 
permit on parcels 6, 8, 9, and 10 by avoiding deep ripping 
jurisdictional waters therein. He sought and received the 
government’s guidance regarding what activities he 
could undertake without a permit. While certain federal 
officials apparently anticipated that he would submit per- 
mit applications for these parcels before he deep ripped 
them it is unclear why he would need a permit if he 
intended to avoid jurisdictional waters. 


Yet the evidence reveals that Tsakopoulos expended 
little effort to comply with the Act prior to the summer of 
1996. It is undisputed there was deep ripping taking 
place on parcels 6 and 10 prior to the preparation and 
verification of a jurisdictional wetlands delineation map, 


App. 114 


which did not take place until the spring and summer of 
that year. Final Pretrial Order filed May 27, 1999, (“the 
Final Pretrial Order”) at 4-5. Without such a map, it is 
unclear how Tsakopoulos’s agents could successfully 
avoid all jurisdictional features. The evidence further 
shows that in September of 1996 some flagging and stak- 
ing was done on the San Joaquin County side, partic- 
ularly around vernal pools. Nevertheless, deep ripping of 
jurisdictional features still occurred. Tsakopoulos testified 
that he relied upon his agents to ensure compliance with 
the government's guidance regarding driving over the 
swales and intermittent drainages. But these agents had 
an unvigilant attitude towards compliance with the Clean 
Water Act on Borden Ranch. Finally, between September 
of 1994 and September of 1996, Tsakopoulos deliberately 
obfuscated his understanding of the Corps’s guidance 
respecting driving over vernal pools and undermined the 
Corps’s enforcement authority by wrongly stating the 
agency gave him confusing guidance as to the nature of 
the contact he could have with jurisdictional waters. He 
knew he was not authorized to deep rip any jurisdictional 
features.19 These findings weigh against good faith. 
While confusion did exist as to whether Tsakopoulos 
needed a permit prior to deep ripping outside jurisdic- 
tional features, Tsakopoulos understood his responsibility 


19 The government's internal discussions about the extent 
of its authority under the Clean Water Act, and the portion of 
that debate that surfaced at one of Tsakopoulos’s meetings with 
federal officials, are irrelevant since Tsakopoulos knew that the 
Corps’s direction to him about his activities on jurisdictional 
waters was clear and consistent. 


App. 115 


to avoid federal jurisdictional features as early as Septem- 
ber of 1994. Yet some of his agents failed to avoid those 
features. For instance, the evidence demonstrates that 
there was no serious effort to avoid waters of the United 
States when deep rippers plowed nearly all of parcel 10. 
This is indicative of the absence of a good faith attempt to 
comply with the Act. This factor favors the imposition of 
a significant civil penalty. 


e. 
The Economic Impact of the Penalty on the Violator 


Another factor concerns the economic impact a civil 
penalty would have on Tsakopoulos. However, he has 
entered into a stipulation with the EPA, filed under seal 
March 12, 1999, wherein he agrees in substance that the 
economic impact of a penalty on him need not be consid- 
ered in determining whether the civil penalty imposed 
should depart from the maximum allowed under the 
Clean Water Act. Further, in the Final Pretrial Order, 
Tsakopoulos agreed that he “has the financial resources 
to respond to any judgment which the Court may make.” 
Id. at 21. Therefore, this factor does not affect the severity 
of the penalty. 


f. 
Other Matters Required by Justice 


Further, section 1319(d) requires consideration of 
“such other matters as justice may require” in fashioning 
a civil penalty. The EPA argues that this Court ought to 
consider the need to deter “the regulated community” 


App. 116 


from violating the Clean Water Act through the imposi- 
tion of a severe civil penalty. EPA’s Trial Brief at 36. 
Otherwise, the EPA argues, the government's regulatory 
program could be subverted. Tsakopoulos does not dis- 
pute these contentions. This factor is relevant and does 
weigh in favor of the imposition of a more severe civil 
penalty. See United States v. Mun. Ath. of Union Township, 
929 F. Supp. 800, 809 (M.D. Pa. 1996), aff'd, 150 F.3d 259 


(3d Cir. 1998). 


No other matter is asserted to be, or appears to be, 
pertinent to this determination. Consequently, the 
amount of the civil penalty is determined. 


3. 
The Amount of the Civil Penalty 


Although the maximum civil penalty under the stat- 
ute is $8,950,000, the EPA seeks a lesser penalty, and 
injunctive relief that would require Tsakopoulos to make 
appropriate restoration for depriving the United States 
government and its people of wetlands. The EPA’s 
approach bespeaks that public policy interests will be 
served best by restoration on Borden Ranch and/or other 
properties owned by Tsakopoulos. This would foster the 
congressional goal of protecting federal wetlands. “Con- 
gress has determined that ‘the . . . destruction of the 
Nation’s wetlands is causing [such] serious, permanent 
ecological damage,’ . . . that wetlands merit protection by 
laws... . ” United States v. Larkins, 657 F. Supp. 76, 86 
(W.D. Ky. 1987) (quoting Starr or SENATE COMM. ON THE 
ENVIRONMENT, 95TH CONG., 2p Sess., A Lecistative History 
or THE CLEAN Water Act oF 1977, 869-70 (Comm. Print 


App. 117 : 


1978)). The issue is what monetary legal penalty should 
be imposed and whether Tsakopoulos could avoid any 
portion of that penalty by restoring damaged or 
destroyed wetlands. On balance, the statutory factors 
dictate that Tsakopoulos either must pay a stiff civil pen- 
alty or make appropriate restoration for depriving the 
nation of wetlands that support wildlife and endangered 
species. This is consistent with the EPA’s policy of accept- 
ing “consent decree provisions which allow the reduction 
of a civil penalty assessment in recognition of the defen- 
Gant’s undertaking an environmentally beneficial ‘mitiga- 
tion project.’ ” Sierra Club, Inc. v. Elec. Controls Design, 
Inc., 909 F.2d 1350, 1354 n.6 (9th Cir. 1990) (quoting the 
EPA’s “Clean Water Act Penalty Policy for Civil Settle- 
ment Negotiations” (1986)). 


The relative seriousness of Tsakopoulos’s violations 
and his lack of earnest effort to comply with the Act merit 
a significant penalty in order to achieve appropriate retri- 
bution. Tsakopoulos realized his obligation to ensure his 
agents avoided deep ripping federal wetlands but failed 
to devise a ripping operation sufficient to avoid these 
jurisdictional features. The plan developed created a 
plain and obvious risk that others would rip these fea- 
tures because it authorized ripping to occur almost con- 
tinuously without enough supervision, and even in the 
wee hours of the morning and night. The haste involved 
in this ripping activity was driven by a profit motive — to 
make parcels of Borden Ranch conducive for vineyards so 
that sales of the properties could be consummated. The 
economic benefit Tsakopoulos obtained from his haste 
enabled him to realize a quicker sale of deep ripped 


App. 118 


property, which included deep ripped jurisdictional wet- 
lands. Reaping such benefits at the expense of damaging 
rare federal wetlands is intolerable under the Act and 
preponderates toward a significant penalty to achieve the 
goal of deterrence. 


On balance, all the considered factors warrant impo- 
sition of a civil penalty pursuant to 33 U.S.C. § 1319(d). 
Tsakopoulos is fined $1,500,000 for his 358 violations of 
the Clean Water Act. However, Tsakopoulos may suspend 
payment of $1,000,000 of this penalty and reduce it to 
$500,000 if he completes the restoration measures identi- 
fied in the injunctive relief portion of this Order. If 
Tsakopoulos elects to undertake such restoration mea- 
sures, he must file a written notice so stating within 30 
days of the date on which this Order is filed. 


E. 
Injunctive Relief 


The restoration measures favored by the EPA are 
given considerable deference because of its expertise in 
achieving the goals of the Clean Water Act. However, to 
institute those measures Tsakopoulos would have to 
cooperate with the involved federal agencies. Although it 
appears that the cost of the restoration sought is signifi- 
cantly less than the $1,000,000 civil penalty which will be 
held in abeyance should Tsakopoulos elect to do the 
restoration,2° reducing the penalty in favor of restoration 


20 If Tsakopoulos does not elect to do the restoration this 
civil penalty could be used by the EPA to do restoration itself 
elsewhere. 


App. 119 


would be instified under the reasoning that 
Tsakopoulos’s election to do the restoration bears upon 
the necessity of a civil penalty. Ciampitti II, 669 F. Supp. at 
686-87. Should Tsakopoulos voluntarily elect to cooperate 
with these agencies, that would indicate his good inten- 
tion to improve the damage that he allowed his agents to 
cause to jurisdictional features of the United States. As 
observed by the Court in United States v. Ciampitti, 615 F. 
Supp. 116, 125 (D.N.J. 1984), Tsakopoulos’s “good faith in 
preparing and implementing a restoration plan is a rele- 
vant factor in assessing the size or necessity of civil 
penalties.” 


Accordingly, if Tsakopoulos makes the election to 
have $1,000,000 of the imposed civil penalty held in abey- 
ance pending determination of whether he has made such 
restoration, he shall cooperate with the EPA and the 
Corps in arranging for the preparation of a restoration 
plan covering at least four acres in and near Goose Creek, 
Dry Creek, and/or on other appropriate areas on prop- 
erty owned by Tsakopoulos (“the applicable land”).?! 
Four acres is approximately twice the acreage directly 
affected by his unlawful deep ripping activities. In light 
of the indirect impact that violations like those committed 
by Tsakopoulos have on jurisdictional waters down- 
stream, the EPA’s policy is to require restoration at a two- 
to-one ratio. See EPA’s Trial Brief at 39. Tsakopoulos does 
not dispute that, if he had applied for a permit to deep 
rip the 30 jurisdictional features, he would have been 
required to undertake restoration of an area determined 
by using this ratio. Therefore, mitigation on four acres of 


21 The EPA must agree to the site selected for restoration. 


App. 120 


wetlands is appropriate. The restoration plan will be pre- 
pared by an independent environmental consulting firm 
selected by the EPA and will be subject to EPA approval. 
Tsakopoulos shall cooperate in the development of this 
restoration plan by (i) providing access to the applicable 
land, and (ii) reimbursing the environmental consultant 
selected by the EPA for the cost of developing the mitiga- 
tion plan, and (iii) assisting the United States in imple- 
menting this plan.?? To serve the goals of restoration, this 
activity may include fill removal, creation of buffers, 
installation of culverts to channel water flow, and 
revegetation. 


III. 
CONCLUSION 


For the above-stated reasons, the Court finds that 
Angelo K. Tsakopoulos committed three hundred fifty- 
eight (358) violations of the Clean Water Act when he 
caused fill to be discharged into waters of the United 
States on parcels 6, 8, 9, and 10 of Borden Ranch. A civil 
penalty pursuant to 33 U.S.C. § 1319(d) is imposed in the 
amount of one million five hundred thousand dollars 
($1,500,000). If Tsakopoulos files a notice that he elects to 
have held in abeyance his obligation to pay one million 
dollars ($1,000,000) of the civil penalty within thirty (30) 
days of the date this Order is filed, then the EPA shall file 


22 The restoration work should be performed by an 
independent firm selected by the EPA. Tsakopoulos shall 
cooperate in providing access to the applicable land and shall 
pay the costs for implementing the mitigation plan. 


App. 121 


a proposed injunction which encompasses the relief 
described in its Trial Brief and section II.E., supra, within 
sixty (60) days of the date this Order is filed; a Judgment 
would then be entered imposing a five hundred thousand 
dollar ($500,000) civil penalty and an injunction requiring 
restoration. If the restoration is not thereafter completed 
as Ordered, the EPA may file a motion seeking appropri- 
ate relief, which may include the re-imposition of all or 
part of that portion of the civil penalty which was held in 
abeyance. However, if Tsakopoulos does not timely elect 
to undertake the restoration, a Judgment will be entered 
imposing the full one million five hundred thousand 
dollar ($1,500,000) civil penalty.

Plaintiff-Counter-defendant Angelo K. Tsakopoulos 
(“Tsakopoulos”) moves to amend the findings set forth in 
the November 8, 1999, Findings of Fact and Conclusions 
of Law (“Findings and Conclusions”) pursuant to Rule 
52(b) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.! Defendant- 
Counterclaimant United States Environmental Protection 


* This case was determined to be suitable for decision 
without oral argument. L.R. 78-230(h). 


1 Unless otherwise indicated, all references herein to Rules 
are to the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. 


App. 123 


Agency (“the EPA”) opposes the motions.* For the rea- 
sons set forth below, the motions are denied. 


Rule 52(a) “requires findings of fact in judge tried 
actions. The purpose of this rule is so appellate courts 
may have a clear understanding of the basis of the deci- 
sion.” Weyerhaeuser Co. v. Atropos Island, 777 F.2d 1344, 
1352 (9th Cir. 1985) (internal quotation marks and citation 
omitted). A district judge should state “the preliminary 
and basic facts” on which his decision rests. Unt v. Aero- 
space Corp., 765 F.2d 1440, 1444 (9th Cir. 1985) (quoting 
Dalehite v. United States, 346 U.S. 15, 24 n.8 (1953)). Rule 
52(b) provides that, on motion, “the court may amend” 
findings pursuant to Rule 52(a), “or make additional find- 
ings.” “Motions under Rule 52(b) are primarily designed 
to correct findings of fact which are central to the ulti- 
mate decision; the Rule is not intended to serve as a 
vehicle for a rehearing.” Davis v. Mathews, 450 F. Supp. 
308, 318 (E.D. Cal. 1978); accord 9 Moore’s Federal Prac- 
tice 3d § 52.60[s] (1999). 


Tsakopoulos argues that certain more specific find- 
ings are required “to sufficiently and precisely indicate 
and afford a clear understanding of the factual bases and 
grounds for the Court’s ultimate conclusions and deci- 
sion in this matter.” Plaintiff's Memorandum of Points 
and Authorities (“Pltf. Mem.”) at 1-2. First, Tsakopoulos 
requests specification of the precise depths at which the 
deep ripper shanks were dragged through jurisdictional 
features in violation of the Clean Water Act. Id. at 3-4. 


2 Tsakopoulos has filed two motions for more specific 
findings: one on December 2, 1999, and a supplemental motion 
on December 8, 1999. Both are resolved by this Order. 


App. 124 


Since Tsakopoulos fails to articulate the relevance of these 
precise depths, this aspect of the motion is denied. Sec- 
ond, Tsakopoulos seeks particularization of the following 
facts assertedly relevant to his estoppel defense: (i) the 
“circumstances, location and type of [deep ripping] 
equipment” which a certain EPA witness had observed 
with its shanks in the uppermost position, (ii) to which 
government agents Tsakopoulos and/or his agent had 
represented that the deep ripper shanks could be raised 
completely out of the ground, (iii) which specific state- 
ments constituted these representations, and (iv) what 
“specific actions” government agents had undertaken in 
reliance upon these statements. Id. at 4-5. However, the 
Findings and Conclusions sufficiently set forth the facts 
which support the conclusion that Tsakopoulos’s estoppei 
defense had not been proven, see Findings and Conclu- 
sions at 7, 17, 30, and Tsakopoulos has not shown how 
further detail would be pertinent. Third, Tsakopoulos 
asks for clarification of certain facts regarding the exis- 
tence of endangered species in depression 2 which was 
found to have been plowed in violation of the Clean 
Water Act. He seeks specification of (i) whether the Court 
found that depression 2 was a “hydrologically-isolated 
vernal pool,” (ii) whether the Court found any 
“actual .. . presence” of endangered species in the 
feature and/or whether the feature was an “actual or 
potential” habitat for such species, (iii) the “historical 
frequency and duration” of spillage from a proximate 
vernal pool, (iv) over what distance this spillage was 
found to occur, (v) whether endangered species are capa- 
ble of surviving transportation from one vernal pool to 
another via overland storm water flow, and (vi) whether 


App. 125 


depression 2 had specific physical characteristics which 
made it a viable habitat for endangered species. Pitf. 
Mem. at 5-6. The findings regarding depression 2 are 
sufficiently set forth in the Findings and Conclusions and 
patently incorporate some of the additional findings 
which Tsakopoulos now seeks. See Findings and Conclu- 
sions at 20-21. In any event, Tsakopoulos has failed to 
show how more @etail would be necessary and/or rele- 
vant to the factual finding that depression 2 had more 
likely than not served as a habitat for a certain endan- 
gered species. Id. at 21. Therefore, this aspect of 
Tsakopoulos’s motion is denied. 


Tsakopoulos next seeks clarification of the precise 
acreage and the widths of each linear feature into which 
fill was deposited by his deep ripping activities. Pitf. 
Mem. at 6. However, the Court previously expressed that 
“the evidence adduced at trial pertaining to the amount 
of jurisdictional wetlands affected was not clear enough 
to ascertain the exact acreage of land adversely affected.” 
Findings and Conclusions at 36. Further, Tsakopoulos has 
not shown how more specificity is necessary or would be 
relevant. Tsakopoulos also seeks a specific finding of the 
“reasonably approximate amount” of the economic bene- 
fit he derived from violating the Clean Water Act. Pltf. 
Mem. at 6-7. As Tsakopoulos notes, the Court found that 
a “relatively high amount of uncertainty” was involved in 
the calculation of economic benefit in this case. Id. at 6 
(citing Findings and Conclusions at 31 n.12). Further, as 
was explained in the Findings and Conclusions, precise 
numbers are unnecessary. See Findings and Conclusions 
at 38, 40. The EPA’s unrebutted expert testimony that 
Tsakopoulos benefited approximately $1 million was 


= 


App: 126 


based upon facts which Tsakopoulos has not disputed, 
and these facts are set forth sufficiently in the Findings 
and Conclusions. Id. at 38-39 & n.17. Therefore, this 
aspect of the motion is denied. Tsakopoulos also requests 
that the Findings and Conclusfons be made more specific 
regarding how he “depriv[ed] the nation of wetlands that 
support wildlife and endangered species” and why the 
Court concluded his violations were relatively serious. Id. 
at 46. Since he has failed to demonstrate the necessity for 
further explanation of these findings than was stated in 
the Findings and Conclusions, this aspect of the motion is 
also denied. 


Finally, in Tsakopoulos’s supplemental motion, he 
seeks clarification of the phrase “independent environ- 
mental consulting firm.” Id. at 49. Since that phrase has 
been adequately clarified in an Order filed December 17, 
1999, the supplemental motion is moot. 


For the reasons stated, Tsakopoulos’s motions for 
more specific findings are denied.

FINAL ORDER 


(Filed March 3, 2000) 


And Related Actions. 


Nm meee eee ee ee 


On February 28, 2000, the parties jointly submitted a 
[Proposed] Final Judgment to which both sides have 
assented. Because the proposed judgment satisfies the 
directives set forth in the February 14, 2000, Order, the 
stipulated conditions set forth in the [Proposed] Final 
Judgment will issue as part of this Final Order, with one 
change described below. 


The parties propose the issuance of a permanent 
injunction which proscribes Tsakopoulos “from engaging 
in any activity associated with rangeland conversion on 
the Borden Ranch, including deep ripping and discing, 
which results in the discharge from a point source of fill 
or dredged material into jurisdictional waters of the 
United States unless he has first obtained a permit under 


App. 128 


the Clean Water Act authorizing such discharge.” [Pro- 
posed] Final Judgment, { 12. This proposal for a perma- 
nent injunction is rejected because the factors which the 
Court must consider in deciding whether to grant a per- 
manent injunction do not favor the issuance of such an 
injunction. See Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(d); United States v. Laerdal 
Manuf. Corp., 73 F.2d 852, 854-55 (9th Cir. 1995) (setting 
out factors); Federal Election Comm'n v. Furgatch, 869 F.2d 
1256, 1262-64 (9th Cir. 1989) (same). 


“lA] federal judge sitting as chancellor is not 
mechanically obligated to grant an injunction for every 
violation of law.” Weinberger v. Romero-Barcelo, 456 U.S. 
305, 313 (1982) (citing Tennessee Valley Authority v. Hill, 
437 U.S. 153, 193 (1978)). Further, an injunction is not the 
only means of ensuring compliance with the Clean Water 
Act. Id. at 314-15 (noting the availability of fines and 
criminal penalties). “Under [33 U.S.C.] § 1365(a), the dis- 
trict court has discretion to determine which form of 
relief is best suited, in the particular case, to abate current 
violations [of the Clean Water Act] and deter future 
ones.” Friends of Earth, Inc. v. Laidlaw Envtl. Services, __ 
U.S. __, 120 S. Ct. 693, 699 (2000). An award of civil 
penalties premised in part upon the need for deterrence 
coupled with appropriate restoration may satisfactorily 
deter the risk of future violations. See id. (“[A] district 
court in a Clean Water Act citizen suit properly may 
conclude that an injunction would be an excessively 
intrusive remedy, because it could entail continuing 
superintendence of the permit holder’s activities by a 
federal court - a process burdensome to court and permit 
holder alike.”); Nat’! Resources Defense Council, Inc. v. EPA, 
966 F.2d 1292, 1300 (9th Cir. 1992) (“Injunctive relief may 


App. 129 


be inappropriate where it requires constant supervi- 
sion.”). In the Findings of Fact and Conclusions of Law, it 
was found that a $1.5 million civil penalty, or the combi- 
nation of a $500,000 penalty and complete restoration of 
the wetlands damaged by Tsakopoulos’s operations, 
would serve to adequately remedy the government for 
his violations of the Clean Water Act. The restorative 
injunction obviates the need for imposition of an addi- 
tional injunction since it fosters the important federal 
interests involved. Since there has been no showing that 
further acti-"e management by the Court is required to 
achieve those interests, the Court declines to impose an 
additional injunction. Therefore, a permanent injunction 
will not issue.? 


Accordingly, as agreed in the stipulated [Proposed] 
Final Judgment: 


Civil Penalty 


1. Tsakopoulos shall pay a civil penalty of $500,000 
pursuant to 33 U.S.C. § 1319(d) as a consequence of his 
violations of the Clean Water Act on the San Joaquin 
County portion of the Borden Ranch during the period 
1995 to 1997. This payment shall be made to the United 
States Treasury.

entry of Judgment. 


App. 130 


Mitigation

be replaced.

for the work, establish criteria for determining when the

mitigation issues as EPA deems necessary. Fill removal, 
creation of buffers, installation of culverts, and revegeta-

the steps reasonably required to be taken by Tsakopoulos 
in connection with implementation of the mitigation pro-

or to impose deed restrictions with respect to the applica-

water sources necessary for mitigation, and the provision 
of information pertinent to the plan.

EPA before implementation. Within 30 days after approv- 
ing the restoration plan, EPA shall provide a copy to

Tsakopoulos. Any subsequent amendments or supple-

Plaintiff (“Tsakopoulos”) moves for partial summary 
judgment against each Defendant and Defendants cross- 
move for summary judgment pursuant to Federal! Rule of 
Civil Procedure 56. For the reasons that follow, 
Tsakopoulos’s motions are granted in part and denied in 
part, the motion of Defendants American Manufacturers 
Mutual Insurance Company (“AM MIC”) and Lumber- 
mens Mutual Casualty Company ( LMCC”) is granted in 
part and denied in part, and the motion of Defendant 
Reliance Insurance Company (“Reliance”) is granted in 
part and denied in part. 


* This case was determined to be suitable for decision 
without oral argument. L.R. 78-230(h). 


App. 136 


I. 
BACKGROUND 


Tsakopoulos claims breach of contract and breach of 
the implied covenant of good faith and fair dealing 
against AMMIC, LMCC, and Reliance arising out of their 
declining to defend Tsakopoulos in certain litigation with 
the United States Environmental Protection Agency 
(“EPA”) or to indemnify him for a certain aspect af the 
March 3, 2000, judgment entered therein (“Judgment”).? 
Tsakopoulos seeks compensatory damages, declaratory 
relief, attorney’s fees and costs. AMMIC, LMCC, and 
Reliance counterclaim for declaratory relief. 


The following material facts are not in dispute. In 
1993, Tsakopoulos purchased a property (“Borden 
Ranch”) comprising 8,348 acres at the border between 
Sacramento and San Joaquin counties. Between late 1993 
through late 1997, Tsakopoulos caused various portions 
of Borden Ranch to be plowed by his agents and/or 
employees primarily to convert the land for use as vine- 
yards. In May of 1997, he and Borden Ranch Partnership 
(“Partnership”), of which he is the managing partner, 
commenced a federal civil action against the United 
States Army Corps of Engineers and the EPA seeking, 
inter alia, a judgment declaring that Tsakopoulos and the 
Partnership were not required to obtain permits pursuant 
to the Clean Water Act (“CWA”), 33 U.S.C. § 1344, to 
engage in certain plowing activities at Borden Ranch. As 


1 As discussed infra, Reliance actually funded 
Tsakopoulos’s defense, but did so under a reservation of rights. 
Reliance seeks a judgment declaring that it was not obligated to 
defend Tsakopoulos. 


App. 137 


part of its August 6, 1997, Amswer, the EPA advanced a 
counterclaim (“Counterclaim”) against Tsakopoulos alleg- 
ing that, beginning sometime in November of 1994, he 
had violated the Clean Water Act during his plowing 
activities on certain portions of Borden Ranch by causing 
dredged or fill material to be discharged into waters of 
the United States. Specifically, the Counterclaim alleged 
that the soil discharged into jurisdictional waters as a 
result of Tsakopoulos’s plowing constituted a “pollutant” 
within the meaning of the CWA.? Both sides thereafter 
filed motions for summary judgment concerning, inter 
alia, whether soil is a pollutant within the meaning of the 
CWA; the August 3, 1998, Amended Order heid that any 
dredged or fill material discharged into waters of the 
United States resulting from plowing would constitute 
the discharge of a “pollutant” under the CWA. 


On October 5, 1998, Tsakopoulos tendered the

sion from Reliance; on that date he tendered the defense 
of the Counterclaim to AMMIC and LMCC, which had 
issued him CGL insurance and excessive coverage poli- 
cies which went into effect on July 16, 1997. In a letter 
dated March 18, 1999, AMMIC and LMCC rejected

rock, sand, cellar dirt and industrial, municipal, and

Tsakopoulos’s tender on the ground that their policies

Tsakopoulos was fined $1.5 million under CWA’s civil

Final Order filed March 3, 2000, at 4.5 Tsakopoulos is

its costs and expenses in preparing and implementing the

Il. 
DUTY TO DEFEND 


The parties agree that California law governs the 
subject insurance policies. Under California law, “a lia- 
bility insurer owes a broad duty to defend its insured 
against claims that create a potential for indemnity. . . . 
[T]he carrier must defend a suit which potentially seeks 
damages within the coverage of the policy. . . . Implicit in 
this rule is the principle that the duty to defend is 
broader than the duty to indemnify. . . . ” Standun, Inc. v. 
Fireman's Fund Ins. Co., 62 Cal. App. 4th 882, 888 (1998) 
(internal quotation marks and citations omitted). “(T]he 
duty to defend should be fixed by the facts which the 
insurer learns from the language of the insurance policy 
and the allegations in the complaint], the insured, or 
other sources. An insurer, theretore, bears a duty to 
defend its insured whenever it ascertains facts which give 
rise to the potential of liability under the policy.” Gray v. 
Zurich Ins. Co., 65 Cal. 2d 263, 276-77 (1966). “To pre- 
vail... on the issue of duty to defend, the insured must 
prove the existence of a-potential for coverage, while the 
insurer must establish the absence of any such potential. 
‘In other words, the insured need only show that the 
underlying claim may fall within policy coverage; the 
insurer must prove it cannot.’ ” Standun, 62 Cal. App. 4th 
at 888-89 (quoting Montrose Chemical Corp. of California v. 
Superior Court (Canadian Universal Ins. Co.), 6 Cal. 4th 287, 
300 (1993)). 


App. 141 


Tsakopoulos seeks a declaration that each Defendant 
was and is obligated to defend him on the Counterclaim and 
summary adjudication on the issue of AMMIC’s and 
LMCC’s liability for breach of contract. Defendants argue 
that they had no duty to defend Tsakopoulos on the Coun- 
terclaim because (i) the alleged discharges did not constitute 
an “occurrence” within the meaning of the policies, (ii) the 
Counterclaim did not seek “damages” within the meaning of 
the policies, and (iii) the Counterclaim arose out of damage 
caused by “pollutants” within the meaning of the “total 
pollution exclusions” in the policies. LMCC contends it had 
no duty to defend Tsakopoulos because his primary insur- 
ance coverage had not been exhausted. These contentions are 
addressed seriatim.® 


A. 


“Occurrence” / Expected or Intended Damages 


Each of the insurance policies at issue sets forth that 
it only covers damages arising out of an “occurrence,” 
which is defined as an “accident”; damages which 
are “intended” or “expected” by the insured are excluded 
from coverage.” Defendants submit no evidence demon- 
strating that Tsakopoulos subjectively “expected or 


6 Since both sides have filed cross motions for summary 
adjudication as to Tsakopoulos’s duty-to-defend claim, and 
certain other issues in this lawsuit, the ordinary implication is, 
and the parties agree, that no disputed issues of material fact 
exist as to these issues and that the disputes over these issues 
may be decided a matter of law. See A & A Concrete, Inc. v. White 
Mountain Apache Tribe, 781 F.2d 1411, 1417 n.1 (9th Cir. 1986). 


7 In each of the subject policies, “occurrence” is defined as 
“an accident, including continuous or repeated exposure to

B. 
“Damages” 


Defendants next contend that the restoration costs 
sought by the government in the Counterclaim were not 
damages within the meaning of the subject policies 
because, at common law, an injunction is an equitable 
remedy, while “damages” in an insurance policy typically 
refers only to legal relief. The term “damages” is not 
defined in the policies. Under California law, at least 
some “costs incurred to comply with an injunction man- 
dating [environmental] cleanup or to reimburse a govern- 
ment agency for cleanup expenses the agency has 
incurred are ‘damages’ within the meaning of the [stan- 
dard CGL] insurance policy.” Intel Corp. v. Hartford Acci- 
dent & Indem. Co., 952 F.2d 1551, 1563 (9th Cir. 1991) 


insured tenders [his] claim for the defense.” Reese v. Travelers 
Ins. Co., 129 F.3d 1056, 1060 (9th Cir. 1997). Since the evidence 
adduced at trial does not establish that Tsakopoulos’s violations 
of the CWA were intentional, Defendants’ failure to adduce any 
other evidence of Tsakopoulos’s knowledge or intent is 
dispositive. 


App. 145 


(citing AIU Ins. Co. v. Superior Court (FMC Corp.), 51 Cal. 
3d 807 (1990) (“AIU”)). Here, the Counterclaim sought, in 
pertinent part, an Order “requir[ing] Tsakopoulos to pre- 
pare and implement a plan for the removal of discharged 
pollutants and restoration of waters of the United States 
on the portions of Borden Ranch in San Joaquin County, 
and to mitigate for the damage caused to the effected 
areas by the unlawful discharges of dredged or fill mate- 
rial.” Tsakopoulos seeks indemnification only for the cost 
of the restorative aspect of the injunction, which requires 
him to reimburse the expenses of an independent envi- 
ronmental consultant designated by the government. 
Defendants argue that Intel Corp. and AIJU are distin- 
guishable because these cases dealt with injunctions 
issued under the Comprehensive Environmental 
Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (“CERCLA”). 
As the California Supreme Court noted in AJU, CERCLA 
authorizes an enforcing agency to seek and recover either 
injunctive restorative relief requiring a liable person to 
cleanup pollution or “reimbursement” for cleanup costs 
incurred by the government. See 51 Cal. 3d at 828, 837-38. 
It was reasoned that, since awards of reimbursement 
were “damages” within the meaning of CGL insurance 
policies, “it would exalt form over substance to interpret 
CGL policies to cover one remedy over the other.” Id. at 
840. Defendants point out that, under the CWA, the gov- 
ernment may seek only civil penalties or injunctive relief. 
They assert that civil penalties under the CWA, for which 
Tsakopoulos does not seek reimbursement, are not “dam- 
ages,” and thus there is no reason to deem the costs of 
complying with injunctive relief imposed under the CWA 


App. 146 


to be “damages” under the CGL insurance policies. This 
argument is unpersuasive. As the Ninth Circuit noted in 
Intel Corp., the California Supreme Court “rejected the 
argument that the distinctions or definitions contained in 
CERCLA with regard to the types of available relief were 
relevant to its determination: at issue was the interpreta- 
tion of the insurance policy under state law.” 952 F.2d at 
1563. The California Supreme Court's holding that injunc- 
tions requiring environmental cleanup are “damages” 
under state law turned on the “purposes addressed” 
through the imposition of the injunctive releif. AJU, 51 
Cal. 3d at 838; see also id. at 841 (“Under CERCLA and 
similar statutes, injunctive relief and reimbursement of 
response costs serve substantially the same purpose.”). 
Therefore, predicting California law, the phrase “dam- 
ages” in the relevant policies emcompasses injunctive 
restorative relief imposed under the CWA. 


There is an additional consideration which neither 
side addresses and which supports this conclusion. The 
portion of the “pollution exclusion” in each policy which 
specifically excludes any “loss, cogt or expense arising 
out of any . . . order that any insured or others test for, 


© Although neither side notes this fact, AJU involved 
injunctive relief imposed not only under CERCLA, but also 
under the CWA and other environmental statutes. 51 Cal. 3d at 
815, 816. Further, the California Supreme Court’s holding 
therein was that “the costs of injunctive relief under the statutes 
in question here are ‘damages’ for CGL purposes.” Id. at 841-42 
(emphasis added). It is unclear whether this language refers in 
part to the CWA. In any event, given the reasoning employed by 
the California Supreme Court, the injunctive remedy imposed 
upon Tsakopoulos constituted “damages” within the meaning 
of the relevant CGL insurance policies under California law.

monitor, clean up, remove, contain, treat, detoxify or 
neutralize, or in any way respond to, or assess the effects 
of pollutants” would be rendered largely superfluous if 
such “!ossies], cost[s], or expense[s]” were not “dam- 
ages” otherwise covered under the policy. An insurance 
policy provision should not be construed so that another 
pelicy provision is rendered mere surplusage. Cat. Cw. 
Cope § 1641; Berg v. MTC Elec. Tech. Co., 61 Cal. App. 4th 
349, 361-62 (1998); Farmers Ins. Exchange v. Knopp, 50 Cal. 
App. 4th 1415, 1421 (1996). 


c. 


Pollution Exclusions 


Defendants next argue that liability for the damages 
Tsakopoulos caused to waters of the United States is 
excluded from coverage under the “total pollution exclu- 
sion” in each policy. However, this exclusion applies only 
to damages caused by “pollutants,” a term which is 
defined in each policy as “any solid, liquid, gaseous or 
thermal irritant or contaminant, including smoke, vapor, 
soot, fumes, acids, alkalis, chemicals and waste.” Defen- 
dants argue that the term “pollutants” unambiguously 
includes soil displaced by Tsakopoulos’s plowing opera- 
tions. Tsakopoulos counters that, in this context, the term 
pollutants is at least ambiguous and, therefore, should be 
construed against Defendants.!! 


™ Because the definition of “pollutants” is ambiguous in 
this context, Tsakopoulos’s alternative contention that the 
pollution exclusion should be construed as applying only to 
“man-made” materials is not reached. 


App. 148 


Under California law, “[aJn insurer may select the 
risks it will insure end those it will not, and a clear 
exclusion will be respected. .. . Where the exclusion is 
clear, [the court] will not rewrite the insurance contract to 
impose coverage where none was contemplated... . 
However, an exclusion or limitation on coverage must be 
clearly stated and will be strictly construed against the 
insurer.” Smith Kandal Real Estate v. Continental Cas. Co., 
67 Cal. App. 4th 406, 414 (1998) (citations omitted); see 
also Manneck v. Lawyers Title Ins. Corp., 28 Cal. App. 4th 
1294, 1302 (1994) (“[I]nsurance contract provisions relat- 
ing to exclusions or exceptions from the performance of 
the basic, underlying obligations are construed strictly 
against the insurer and liberally in favor of the 
insured. ...”). A term in an insurance policy is deer ..u 
ambiguous if “it is capable of two or more constructions, 
both of which are reasonable.” Fireman's Fund Ins. Co. v. 
Superior Court (Vickers Inc.), 65 Cal. App. 4th 1205, 1212 
(1997), review dismissed 79 Cal. Rptr. 2d 72 (1998) (“Fire- 
man’s Fund”). “Of course, as is true of any contract, [the 
court] must interpret the policy as a whole, not in the 
abstract, and [the court] will not strain to create an ambi- 
guity where none exists or indulge in tortured construc- 
tions to divine some theoretical ambiguity in order to 
find coverage where none was contemplated.” Id. at 
1212-13 (citations omitted); see also Manneck, 28 Cal. App. 
4th at 1302. 


As noted, a substance must be a “contaminant” or an 
“irritant” to be a “pollutant” within the meaning of the 
policies. Webster’s Third New International Dictionary 
(1986) (“Webster’s”) defines a contaminant as “something 
that contaminates”; to “contaminate” means “to soil, 


App. 149 


stain, corrupt, or infect by contact or association” or 
“make inferior or impure by mixture,” or “to render unfit 
for use by introduction of unwholesome or undesirable 
elements.” Webster’s at 491.12 An “irritant” is “something 
that irritates or excites,” and to “irritate” means “to 
increase the action of” or “heighten excitement in” some- 
thing. Id. at 1197. Soil discharged into previously- 
unspoiled waters of the United States might constitute a 
“contaminant” or an “irritant” if those terms were to be 
construed liberally. However, the definition of “pollu- 
tants” in each policy sets forth specific examples of con- 
taminants and/or irritants: “smoke, vapor, soot, fumes, 
acids, alkalis, chemicals and waste.” According to the 
principle of ejusdem generis, the proper construction of the 
term “pollutant” is therefore “restricted” to contaminants 
and irritants which are “of the same kind, class, or 
nature” as the specific examples listed. Harris v. Capital 
Growth Investors XIV, 52 Cal. 3d 1142, 1159 (1991); see also 
Mez Indus., Inc. v. Pacific Nat'l Ins. Co., 76 Cal. App. 4th 
856, 873 (1999) (“Our construction of [a phrase in an 
insurance policy] is informed by the doctrine of ejusdem 
generis under which a term is interpreted by reference to 
the surrounding language.”).! Defendants argue that the

legal meaning.” Cat. Civ. Cope § 1644; see also Stanford Ranch, 
Inc. v. Maryland Cas. Co., 89 F.3d 618, 626 (9th Cir. 1996). Resort 
to a dictionary is often appropriate to find a word’s ordinary 
and popular meaning. See Scott v. Continental Ins. Co., 44 Cal. 
App. 4th 24, 29-32 (1999) (citing, among other sources, 
Webster’s). 


8 Although Harris involved the construction of a general 
term which followed a list of specific terms, “[e]jusdem generis

use of the word “including” before the specific examples 
means that the definition of “pollutants” should not be 
limited to substances which are similar to specified exam- 


ples. However, the use of the word “including” does not — 


affect the application of the ejusdem generis doctrine. 
County of Yolo v. Los Rios Community College Dist., 5 Cl. 
App. 4th 1242, 1254 (1992) (holding that the term 
“include, but need does not be limited to” does not 
preclude the application of the ejusdem generis principle). 


Defendants next contend that certain California 
courts have held that the absolute pollution exclusion is 
unambiguous. See, e.g., Titan Corp. v. Aetna Cas. & Surety 
Co., 22 Cal. App. 4th 457, 469-70 (1994) (“We are unaware 
of any California cases holding that a similarly worded 
exclusion was ambiguous as to its coverage.”) (finding 
that trichloroethylene (TCE) is a “pollutant”).1¢ But insur- 
ance policy provisions “can, of course, be ambiguous in 
one context and not another.” Continental Cas. Co. v. 
Rapid-American Corp., 80 N.Y.2d 640, 652 (1993) (noting 
that the pollution exclusion has been held to be unam- 


biguous in covering discharges of “acids, alkalis and toxic 


chemicals,” but ambiguous as to discharges of “natural 


applies whether specific words follow general words... or vice 
versa. In either event, the general term or category is ‘restricted 
to those things which are enumerated specifically.’ ” Harris, 52 
Cal. 3d at 1160 n.7 (quoting Martin v. Holiday Inns, Inc., 199 Cal. 
App. 3d 1434, 1437 (1988)). 


14 Notably, however, Titan Corp. found that “[t]he relevant 
policy language [comprising the pollution exclusion] may be 
ambiguous on whether [discarded] scrap ferrite, brick, rubble, 
[or] wood debris [would] fall within the exclusionary 
language.” 22 Cal. App. 4th at 470 (emphasis added). 


App. 151 


materials”) (citing Hicks v. American Resources Ins. Co., 544 
So. 2d 952, 953 (Ala. 1989), and Molton, Allen & Williams v. 
St. Paul Fire & Marine Ins. Co., 347 So. 2d 95, 99 (Ala. 1977)

Ins. Co., 79. Cal. App. 4th 105, 112 (2000) (“Contract terms 
are not ambiguous in a vacuum; they must be examined 
in context.”); see also Titan Corp., 22 Cal. App. 4th at 470. 
Molton is persuasive that the pollution exclusions at issue 
are ambiguous in this context.5 In Molton, the insured 
was a developer who was constructing roads in a new 
development situated uphill from neighboring parcels. 
347 So. 2d at 96. After the roadways were cleared and cut, 
but before they were paved, rainfall washed over the 
roadways and carried “sandstone and sandstone-type 
materials found naturally in the earth,” which the rain- 
water had broken down into sand, onto the neighboring 
parcels, damaging them. Id. at 96-97. The Court rejected 
the insurer’s argument that a pollution exclusion sub- 
stantially similar to the ones in this case eliminated cover- 
age for the damage to the neighboring parcels, stating: 


[WJhile a liberal construction of the ‘pollution 
exclusion’ would include the damage allegedly

inapposite because that case “involved a pollution exclusion 
which, unlike the AMMIC policy, failed to define ‘pollutants’ at 
all.” AMMIC’s & LMCC’s Memorandum of Points and

misleading since the pollution exclusion in Molton was nearly

Defendants argue that California courts have rejected the 
notion that the pollution exclusion is limited to hazard- 
ous or toxic materials and suggest that Molton found an 
ambiguity based upon a finding that the sand in that case 
was not hazardous. However, this misreads the holding 
of Molton, which found that pollution exclusion was 
ambiguous in the context of sand, given the specific 
examples of “irritants, contaminants or pollutants” listed 
in the policy. 347 So. 2d at 97. 


In contrast to Molton, Defendants cite Pennsylvania 
Nat'l Mut. Cas. Ins. Co. v. Triangle Paving, Inc., 973 F. Supp. 
560 (E.D.N.C. 1996), aff'd, 121 F.3d 699 (4th Cir. 1997) 
(Table) (“Triangle Paving”), which held that “sediment 
dislodged by the [insured’s] construction activity [which] 
escaped the construction site and contaminated down- 
stream water located on private property” constituted a 
“pollutant” within the meaning of a substantially similar 
pollution exclusion in a CGL insurance policy. 973 
F. Supp. at 562. However, Defendants have not shown 
that Triangle Paving is persuasive guidance for interpret- 
ing the exclusion clauses in this case. First, the district 


App. 153 


court in that case rejected the argument that “the enumer- 
ated list in the definition serves to limit pollutants to the 
specified examples,” and held that, “[iJn context, the term 
‘including’ clearly signifies that the ensuing list [of speci- 
fied pollutants] is not one of limitation.” Id. at 563. But 
under California law, the doctrine of ejusdem generis calls 
for a contrary analysis of the policies in this case. Harris, 
52 Cal. 3d at 1159-60; Los Rios Community College Dist., 5 
Cal. App. 4th at 1254. The district court in Triangle Paving 
also noted that North Carolina’s Sedimentation Pollution 
Control Act expressed the State legislature’s finding that 
“(t]he sedimentation of streams, lakes and other waters of 
this State constitutes a major pollution problem.” 973 
F. Supp. at 564 (quoting N.C. Gen. Stat. § 113A-51). 
Accordingly, the district court concluded that “the statu- 
tory scheme offers uncontroverted evidence of how sedi- 
mentation is perceived and treated in the very profession 
in which defendant is engaged.” Id. at 565. Defendants 
point out that, similarly, the definition of “pollutants” 
under the CWA includes soil discharged during plowing. 
However, under California law, the determination 
whether a clause is ambiguous depends upon whether a 
clause is subject to two or more reasonable interpreta- 
tions; a court must look to the provision’s “plain meaning 
or the meaning a layperson would ordinarily attach to it.” 
Fireman's Fund, 65 Cal. App. 4th at 1212-13; see also Cul- 
ligan v. State Compensation Ins. Fund, 81 Cal. App. 4th 429, 
434 (1999). 


Since the definition of “pollutants” contained in each 
of the relevant policies is reasonably construed either to 
include soil discharged into waters of the United States or 


App. 154 


to exclude such material, that term is ambiguous under 
the circumstances of this case. Neither construction 
would be “tortuous,” Fireman's Fund, 65 Cal. App. 4th at 
1212; “strange,” Culligan, 81 Cal. App. 4th at 435; or 
“absurd,” id.; each would be reasonable. While soil could 
arguably be an “irritant” or a “contaminant” when dis- 
charged into previously-unspoiled waters, the list of 
specified examples of pollutants which follows these 
terms restricts their construction. Since soil is not appar- 
ently of “the same kind, class, or nature,” Harris, 52 Cal. 
3d at 1159, as “smoke, vapor, soot, fumes, acids, alkalis, 
chemicals [or] waste,” the definition of pollutant does not 
unambiguously cover soil. 


Under California law, an ambiguity in an insurance 
policy “is resolved by interpreting the ambiguous provi- 
sions in the sense the promisor (i.e., the insurer) believed 
the promisee understood them at the time of formation.” 
Montrose Chem. Corp. of California v. Admiral Ins. Co., 10 
Cal. 4th 645, 667 (1995) (quoting AJU, 51 Cal. 3d at 822); 
see also Cat. Crv. Cope § 1649. “This rule, as applied to a 
promise of coverage in an insuramce policy protects not 
the subjective beliefs of the insurer but, rather, the objec- 
tively reasonable beliefs of the insured.” Id. (quoting Bank 
of the West v. Superior Court (Indus. Indem. Co.), 2 Cal. 4th 
1254, 1265 (1992)) (internal quotation marks and citation 
omitted) “Only if this rule does not resolve the ambiguity 
[does the court] then resolve it against the insurer.” Id. 
(quoting Bank of the West, 2 Cal. 4th at 1265); see also AIU, 
51 Cal. 3d at 822; Car. Civ. Cope § 1654. 


Defendants contend that Tsakopoulos could not rea- 
sonably have expected the relevant policies to cover soil 


App. 155 


discharged into waters of the United States because of his 
familiarity with the CWA and his repeated interactions 
with government agencies which enforce the CWA and 
which consistently had asserted that the CWA required 
that Tsakopoulos obtain a permit for soil discharges 
which might occur during his plowing activities. 
Tsakopoulos counters that the definition of “pollutant” 
set forth in the CWA should have no impact on the 
construction of the term “pollutant” contained in the 
relevant CGL insurance policies, citing Legarra v. Federated 
Mut. Ins. Co., 35 Cal. App. 4th 1472 (1995). In Legarra, the 
insureds contended that petroleum was not a pollutant 
under their CGL insurance policy’s pollution exclusion 
based upon a statutory exclusion for petroleum under a 
particular California hazardous-substances law. Id. at 
1481. The court rejected this argument because the insur- 
ance policy defined the term “pollutant” differently than 
the cited statutes, and petroleum is widely held to be a 
“contaminant” or “irritant.” Id. Similar reasoning applies 
here. Defendants have not shown how the CWA’s defini- 
tion of “pollutant” has any relevance to the reasonable- 
ness of Tsakopoulos’s expectation of coverage under the 
policies. Although Defendants argue that Tsakopoulos’s 
knowledge of the government’s interpretation of the 
CWA is relevant to the objective reasonableness of his 
construction of the insurance policies, that argument fails 
to adequately consider how Tsakopoulos could have rea- 
sonably interpreted the disputed definition of, “pollu- 
tants” in this context, and the intended function of the 


. pollution exclusion in the policies. Defendants have not 


shown that Tsakopoulos’s expectation of coverage under 
the CGL insurance policies was objectively unreasonable. 


App. 156 


Since coverage for the unpermitted discharges of soil “is 
consistent with the insured’s objectively reasonable 
expectations” of coverage, the ambiguity of the definition 
of “pollutants” in this context must be resolved against 
Defendants. Bank of the West, 2 Cal. 4th at 1265; see also 
Cat. Civ. Cope § 1654. 


D. 
LMCC’s Duty as Excess Insurance Provider 


LMCC argues that, in any event, it owed no duty to 
defend Tsakopoulos on the Counterclaim until “the 
AMMIC policy and the Reliance policy issued to 
Tsakopoulos have been exhausted through judgments or 
settlements.” AMMIC’s & LMCC’s Memorandum of 
Points and Authorities filed June 1, 2000, at 18. 
Tsakopoulos does not respond to this contention. “Unless 
the provisions of an excess policy provide otherwise, an 
excess insurer has no obligation to provide a defense to 
its insured before the primary coverage is exhausted.” 
Community Redevelopment Agency v. Aetna Cas. & Sur. Co., 
50 Cal. App. 4th 329, 338 (1996) (citing Hartford Accident & 
Indemnity Co. v. Superior Court (Syntex Corp.), 23 Cal. App. 
4th 1774, 1779-80 (1994)). Since Tsakopoulos has not 
shown that his primary insurance coverage has been 
exhausted, LMCC does not owe a duty to defend the 
Counterclaim. 


App. 157 


E. 


Conclusion 


Since Tsakopoulos has shown there existed “a poten- 
tial for coverage” for “damages” sought by the Counter- 
claim, and Defendants AMMIC and Reliance have failed 
to demonstrate “the absence of any such potential,” these 
Defendants owe Tsakopoulos a duty to defend him on the 
Counterclaim. Standun, 62 Cal. App. 4th at 888-89. Fur- 
ther, by failing to defend Tsakopoulos after he tendered 
the defense of the Counterclaim, AMMIC breached the 
insurance policy it issued to him. Tsakopoulos is entitled 
to a declaratory judgment that AMMIC and Reliance 
were obligated to defend him on the Counterclaim after 
the defense had been tendered to the respective insurer, 
and to summary adjudication of his breach-of-contract 
claim against AMMIC. Since Tsakopoulos has not shown 
that his primary insurance has been exhausted, LMCC is 
entitled to summary judgment on Tsakopouips’s duty-to- 
defend claims. Tsakopoulos has not shown that Reliance 
breached the duty to defend arising from its insurance 
policy, because it provided funding for the defense under 
a reservation of rights. Therefore, the aspect of Reliance’s 
motion which seeks summary judgment on Tsakopoulos’s 
breach-of-contract claim against it will be granted.’ 


16 In his papers, Tsakopoulos indicates that he would 
dismiss his claims for breach of contract and breach of the 
implied covenant of good faith and fair dealing against Reliance 
following resolution of these motions. Tsakopoulos’s 
Memorandum of Points and Authorities filed May 31, 2000, at 6 
n.1. Since Reliance seeks summary judgment on these claims in 
its motion, the claims are resolved herein. 


App. 158 


Il. 
DUTY TO INDEMNIFY 


Reliance agreed to indemnify Tsakopoulos for any 
damages he became legally obligated to pay arising out 
of covered occurrences through July 15, 1997. AMMIC 
agreed to indemnify him for occurrences on or after July 
16, 1997. LMCC provides excess insurance coverage for 
occurrences on or after July 16, 1997. Tsakopoulos seeks a 
declaratory judgment that all Defendants are obligated to 
indemnify him for the restorative injunction aspect of the 
Judgment entered in Borden Ranch. 


A. 
AMMIC’s Liability 

“The general rule is long-settled in California that ‘an 
insurer that wrongfully refuses to defend is liable on the 
judgment against the insured.’” Pershing Park Villas 
Homeowners Assoc. v. United Pacific Ins. Co., __ F.3d __, 
2000 WL 914153, at *5 (9th Cir. July 10, 2000) (quoting 
Gray, 65 Cal. 2d at 279). Since AMMIC breached its duty 
to defend Tsakopoulos on the Counterclaim, Tsakopoulos 
contends AMMIC is automatically obligated to indemnify 
him for the costs of the restorative injunction. AMMIC 
does not respond to this contention. However, it argues in 
a footnote that “Tsakopoulos acknowledges that the 
[Findings and Conclusions] ‘ultimately found no viola- 
tions by TSAKOPOULOS occurred after January, 1997.’ 
While this may not impact AMMIC’s duty to defend, it 
certainly impacts its duty to indemnify since [its] policy 
did not become effective until approximately seven (7) 
months after the last violation of the Clean Water Act. 


App. 159 


Even if the Court determiens that coverage exists for 
violations of the Clean Water Act, AMMIC cannot have 
any indemnity obligations for violations of the Clean 
Water Act, none of which take place during its policy 
period.” AMMIC’s & LMCC’s Memorandum of Points 
and Authorities filed June 15, 2000, at 22-23 n.5 (emphasis 
in original). Tsakopoulos does not dispute that no CWA 
violations after January of 1997 are at issue. 
Tsakopoulos’s Memorandum of Points and Authorities 
filed May 31, 2000, at 18 n.3; Tsakopoulos’s Memorandum 
of Points and Authorities filed June 14, 2000, at 28; 
Tsakopoulos’s Reply Memorandum filed June 23, 2000, at 
10.!7 


Since AMMIC wrongfully refused to defend 
Tsakopoulos on the Counterclaim, it is liable for “the 
amount which will compensate [Tsakopoulos] for all the 
detriment proximately caused [by its breach], or which, 
in the ordinary course of things, would be likely to result 
therefrom.” Amato v. Mercury Cas. Co., 53 Cal. App. 4th 
825, 831 (1997) (quoting Cav. Crv. Cove § 3300). Where, as 
here, an insured is able to mount a defense notwithstand- 
ing “the insurer’s refusal to defend, the usual contract 
damages are the cost of the defense.” Id. However, where 
the insurer tortiously refuses to defend — i.e., breaches the 


17 In the Findings and Conclusions, the undersigned 
district judge found that the EPA had not sustained the burden 
of demonstrating that any of the CWA violations occurred 
subsequent to January 30, 1997, the date after which the civil 
penalty for violations of the CWA increased by ten percent. 
Findings and Conclusions at 34. However, it was noted that 
there was evidence which suggested there might have been 
CWA violations as late as April of 1997. Id. 


App. 160 


implied covenant of good faith and fair dealing - it is 
generally obligated to indemnify the insured for the sub- 
sequent judgment “and cannot rely on hindsight that a 
subsequent lawsuit establishes noncoverage.” Id. at 833; 
see also Pershing Park Villas Homeowners Assoc., 2000 WL 
914153, at *5. Here, Tsakopoulos has not yet shown that 
AMMIC tortiously refused to provide for a defense on the 
counterclaim. Therefore, he is not entitled to a declaration 
that AMMIC is obligated to indemnify him for any part of 
the Judgment. However, AMMIC is not entitled to sum- 
mary adjudication on this issue either because, as dis- 
cussed infra, it has failed to demonstrate that it did not 
act in bad faith in refusing Tsakopoulos’s tender of the 
defense. 


B. 
LMCC’s Liability 


Since Tsakopoulos does not dispute that there were 
no CWA violations found after January of 1997, and 
LMCC did not breach any duty to defend him on the 
Counterclaim, Tsakopoulos has not shown that LMCC is 
liable to indemnify him for any part of the Judgment. 


Cc. 


“Occurrence” 


Defendants argue in opposition to Tsakopoulos’s 
claims for indemnification that the Findings and Conclu- 
sions imposed liability for CWA violations which do not 
constitute an “occurrence” within the coverage of the 
policies. But as discussed supra, Defendants have not 
shown that the discharges of soil which led to 


App. 161 


Tsakopoulos’s liability under the CWA were expected or 
intended. Although the Findings and Conclusions evince 
that Tsakopoulos acted in gross dereliction of his duties 
under the CWA, Defendants point to no evidence that he 
acted with the requisite knowledge or intent such as 
would deprive him of coverage under the CGL insurance 
policies. The restorative injunction was not based upon 
any finding that Tsakopoulos acted intentionally or 
knowingly in discharging soil into jurisdictional waters. 
Therefore, Tsakopoulos has shown that the discharges 
constituted an occurrence or occurrences within the cov- 
erage of the policies. 


D. 
“Damages” 


Defendants contend that the restc:ative injunction 
entered in Borden Ranch was imposed in lieu of $1 million 
in civil penalties and thus the injunction should be 
deemed punitive in nature, and not “damages” within the 
coverage of the policies. However, Tsakopoulos’s consent 
to entry of the restorative injunction reflects his agree- 
ment to restore damaged wetlands, or replace any which 
cannot be restored. “In both instances, the amount 
‘restored’ relates to actual harm caused by 
[Tsakopoulos’s] prior actions.” United States v. Telluride 
Co., 146 F.3d 1241, 1248 (10th Cir. 1998).?® Tsakopoulos’s 


18 Although Defendants did not argue that the injunction is 
punitive because the acreage involved in the restoration exceeds 


‘the actual amount found damaged in Borden Ranch, this 


argument would have been unavailing. The excess acreage 
resulted from the government's position that, because 


App. 162 


decision to opt for a restorative injunction in lieu of a 
monetary civil penalty does not change the remedial 
nature of the restorative injunction. A consensual restora- 
tive injunction may constitute “damages” within the 
meaning of the CGL insurance policies. Intel Corp., 952 
F.2d at 1563-64 (concluding that “the California Supreme 
Court would extend [the holding in AJU] and find that 
costs incurred to comply with a consent decree are ‘dam- 
ages’ ”). Thus Defendants have not shown that the resto- 
rative injunction imposed did not constitute damages 
which Tsakopoulos was obligated to pay and for which 
he is be [sic] entitled to indemnification under the rele- 
ant policies. 


E. 


Conclusion 


Since Tsakopoulos has shown that the cost of the 
restorative injunction imposed as part of the Judgment 
constitutes damages which he is legally obligated to pay 
within the meaning of the subject policies, Defendant 
Reliance is obligated to indemnify him for such costs. 
Since he has not yet shown that AMMIC acted in bad 
faith in denying his tender of the defense of the Counter- 
claim, he is not entitled to a declaration that AMMIC is 


Tsakopoulos’s plowing activities caused indirect harm to “areas 
of Goose Creek and other waters on the San Joaquin [County] 
side of Borden Ranch [not directly impacted by the plowing 
activities], e.g., through sedimentation and increased water 
flow[,] compensatory restoration should be at a 2-to-1 ratio.” 
Borden Ranch, Trial Brief of the United States filed August 10, 
1999, at 39. 


App. 163 


liable for indemnification of such costs. LMCC is entitled 
to summary judgment on Tsakopoulos’s indemnification 
claims. 


IV. 
TSAKOPOULOS’S BAD-FAITH CLAIMS 


Defendants seek summary judgment on 
Tsakopoulos’s claims for breach of the implied covenant 
of good faith and fair dealing, arguing solely that they 
may not be heid liable for breaching the covenant because 
they did not breach any contractual duty to Tsakopoulos. 
“|T] here are at least two separate requirements to estab- 
lish breach of the implied covenant: (1) benefits due 
under the policy must have been withheld; and (2) the 
reason for withholding benefits must have been unrea- 
sonable.” Love v. Fire Ins. Exchange, 221 Cal. App. 3d 1136, 
1151 (1990). Since AMMIC did breach its duty to defend 
Tsakopoulos on the Counterclaim, the aspect of its motion 
which seeks summary judgment on Tsakopoulos’s bad- 
faith claim fails. However, Tsakopoulos has not shown 
that either Reliance’? or LMCC have breached any con- 
tractual duty under their policies. Therefore, these Defen- 
dants are entitled to summary judgment on 
Tsakopoulos’s bad-faith claims. 


19 Tsakopoulos apparently acquiesces in this aspect of 
Defendant Reliance’s motion. See note 16, supra. 


App. 164 


V. 
CONCLUSION 


For the reasons stated, Tsakopoulos’s motions are 
granted in part and denied in part, AMMIC’s and 
LMCC’s motion is granted in part and denied in part, and 
Reliance’s motion is granted in part and denied in part. 
Tsakopoulos’s claim for breach of contract against 
AMMIC is SUMMARILY ADJUDICATED in 
Tsakopoulos’s favor. Tsakopoulos’s claims for breach of 
contract, breach of the implied covenant of good faith 
and fair dealing, and declaratory relief against LMCC are 
SUMMARILY ADJUDICATED in LMCC’s favor. 
Tsakopoulos’s claims for breach of contract and breach of 
the implied covenant of good faith and fair dealing 
against Reliance are SUMMARILY ADJUDICATED. in 
Reliance’s favor. It is ADJUDGED AND DECLARED that 
AMMIC and Reliance owe Tsakopoulos a duty to defend 
him on the Counterclaim and that Reliance owes 
Tsakopoulos a duty to indemnify him for his costs in 
complying with the restorative injunction issued in Bor- 
den Ranch. The issues of damages on Tsakopoulos’s 
breach of contract claim against AMMIC and of liability 
and damages on Tsakopoulos’s bad-faith claims against 
AMMIC remain for trial. 


IT IS SO ORDERED. 


DATED: August 8, 2000

I. INTRODUCTION 


1. This Administrative order on Consent (“Consent 
Order” or “Order”) is entered into voluntarily by the 
United States Environmental Protection Agency, Region 9 
(“EPA”), the United States Army Corps of Engineers 
(“USAGE”) and Angelo K. Tsakopoulos (hereinafter 
“Respondent”). Respondent is the owner of real property 
located at an agricultural site known as Borden Ranch in 
Sacramento and San Joaquin Counties, California. EPA 
alleges and Respondent denies that Respondent has dis- 
charged dredged and/or fill material at the Borden Ranch 
Site (“Site”) in violation of Section 301(a) of the Clean 
Water Act (the “Act” or “CWA”), 33 U.S.C.’§ 1311(a), in 
that these activities were discharges of dredged or fill 
material into waters of the United States without authori- 
zation under Section 404 of the Act. 33 U.S.C. § 1344, 
and/or in violation of a permit issued pursuant to Section 


App. 167 


404 of the Act. This Consent Order directs the Respon- 
dent to undertake specified measures to offset the 
adverse environmental impacts and habitat losses occur- 
ring in waters of the United States resulting from the 
alleged unauthorized activities. 


II. JURISDICTION 


1. EPA issues this Consent Order under the authority 
vested in its Administrator by Section 309(a) of the CWA, 
33 U.S.C. § 1319(a). This authority has been delegated to 
the EPA Region 9 Administrator and re-delegated to the 
Director of Water Management Division. USACE is a 
party to this Consent Order because of its authority to 
issue orders requiring compliance with permit conditions 
pursuant to section 404(s) of the Clean Water Act, 33 
U.S.C. § 1344(s). 


2. Respondent agrees not to contest EPA’s and USACE’s 
jurisdiction or authority to enter into or enforce this 
Consent Order. Respondent also agrees not to contest the 
validity of any terms and conditions of this Consent 
order in any action to enforce, or in any action arising 
from, the Order. Specifically, Respondent agrees not to 
contest a determination by the Regional Administrator, 
EPA Region IX and the District Engineer, USACE Sacra- 
mento District, pursuant to Paragraph IX.5., as to the 
final preservation and/or mitigation requirements with 
which Respondent must comply. 


3. EPA’s and USACE’s decisions or actions in entering 
into and pursuant to this Consent Order are not subject to 
judicial review prior to the United States’ initiation of 


App. 168 


judicial action to compel Respondent’s compliance with 
this Consent Order. 


III. DEFINITIONS 


Unless defined herein, terms used in this Consent 
Order shall have the meaning as assigned in the CWA or 
in regulations promulgated by EPA or the USACE under 
the CWA. The following definitions shall apply to this 
Order: 


1. “Consent Order” or “Order” shall mean this docu- 
ment, all attachments hereto, all its subsequent mutually 
agreed to modifications, and all submissions, including, 
but not limited to, deliverables, plans, schedules, reports 
(other than progress reports), maps, technical mem- 
oranda and specifications, which are required by this 
Order and approved by EPA. Upon EPA approval, 
Respondent’s submissions are incorporated and enforce- 
able as part of this Order. In case of inconsistency, this 
document and its subsequent modifications shall control. 


2. “USACE” shall mean the U.S. Army Corps of Engi- 
neers. 


3. “EPA” shall mean the U.S. Environmental Protection 
Agency.

vice.

dent.

period expires on a Saturday, Sunday or federal legal 
holiday, it shall be extended to include the next working 
day.

unauthorized activities through preservation of existing 
environmental values, and which are to be dedicated in a 
conservation easement, as required in Paragraph IX 
below. The 404 Preservation Site is depicted in Attach- 
ment 1 and consists of Parcel 3 and Parcel 26 (outlined in
